► 美國經濟仍具韌性 ，惟通膨壓力猶存 。觀察
美國9月ISM服務業指數表現 ，內需活動維持
穩健，企業投入成本壓力亦有所升溫；另一
方面，受地緣政治因素影響 ，美國柴油價格
大幅攀升 ，而柴油為卡車運輸 、農業及製造
業的重要燃料 ，若價格持續維持高檔 ，恐進
一步增添通膨壓力 。考量聯準會偏鷹立場 ，
且當前通膨降溫進程不如預期 ，年底前仍保
有升息空間。
► 美國債務總額突破40兆美元，預估2026年公
眾持有聯邦債務占 GDP的比率將達 101%，
財政可持續性風險升溫 。與此同時 ，AI基礎

建設舉債熱潮 ，疊加中東局勢動盪帶來的通
膨不確定性 ，迫使投資人重新定價長天期債
券風險 ，要求更高的風險溢酬 。隨實質殖利
率上行，美債10年期殖利率揚升並持續高懸
於5%水準之上，30年期長債殖利率亦同步站
上近20年來高點 。隨長天期債券利率續揚 ，
殖利率曲線趨陡峭 ，債券配置宜先避開長天
期部位，聚焦中短天期債券鎖利機會。

6
投資焦點
► 距離美國期中選舉投票僅剩不到1個月，觀察
Polymarket賭盤預估數據顯示 ，眾議院選情
較為明朗，民主黨有超過9成的機率控制眾議
院。但參議院選情則尚有變數 ，今年以來兩
黨均互有領先 ，雖然數據顯示民主黨掌控參
議院機率呈現上揚 ，然隨時序進入選前最後
衝刺期 ，參議院能否亦由民主黨掌控仍待觀
察。故就選前而言 ，由於不同政黨的政策傾
向差異 ，潛在受惠產業亦有不同 ，選舉結果
的不確定性恐令投資人不易大舉押注股市。
► 另一方面 ，油價高檔徘迴 ，導致年底前通 膨
擔憂仍存 ，加上AI企業發債需求成長 、美國
財政體質惡化等 因素共同發酵 ，推動近期 美
債殖利率逐步攀高 ，並令股市預期本益 比壓
力延續 ，進而限縮了投資人短期對美股的追
價動能，短期指數恐維持震盪輪動格局 。可
留意抗波動 、體質佳且具價值優勢之標的 ，
待期中選舉結束與3Q26財報展開後，不確定
性減退將有助資金回歸長線AI題材。
殖利率攀升令美股本益比面臨壓力參議院期中選舉選情仍具變數
期中選前擾動與殖利率走高，美股短期恐仍震盪輪動

投資焦點日股
► 9月日本銀行決議升息 1碼，明確表示潛在
CPI已接近2%，未來也將繼續調高利率 ，政
策重點開始轉向防範通膨向上偏離目標 。然
因有2位委員反對升息，加上總裁植田和男會
後談話偏向中性 ，令市場解讀此次升息未如
預期鷹派，美日利差不易快速縮窄，故9月會
議後日元反而呈現利多出盡的回貶反應。
► 觀察今年以來日股面臨短線日圓升值的狀態
並未出現顯著走弱 態勢。一方面反映企業治
理改革推動股東報酬 提升已成為 支撐股價的
重要結構性因素 。另一方面 ，升息代表日本
經濟已具備一定的健康程度 ，貨幣政策正常
化將不致對需求帶來衝擊 。因此 ，隨BoJ延
續升息方向 ，將擴大銀行淨利差 ，改善其獲
利結構，並提升金融資產收益 。儘管近期整
體日股預期獲利成長走勢略顯停滯 ，然日本
銀行股 在總 體經 濟 與貨幣 政策環 境的 支持
下，獲利成長有望逆勢攀升 ，料將成為後續
資金關注重點。
日圓升值對日股影響似乎有限，關注銀行股獲利走強BoJ升息方向延續，惟其步伐仍取決於經濟與物價

BoJ政策保持正常化路線，日本銀行股有望持續受惠
投資焦點

### Page 8

8
股市
 公債殖利率走高雖可能壓抑 股債市場近期 動能，然而觀察美股第三季獲利年增 率有望仍為雙位數成
長，或支撐股市表現 。市場持續關注AI資本支出是否帶來獲利成長 ，短期內評價面壓力將增添市場
動盪，可分散配置於不同國家與產業 ，降低市場波動影響 。總結上述，地緣衝突、殖利率偏高及評
價面壓力等紛擾 帶來干擾，可布局防禦 股增強抗震力 。長期而言，科技及AI半導體發展趨勢不變且
基本面佳，可逢拉回時分批布局。
 歐、日股可作為分散配置重點 ，加上全球利率走揚有利銀行股 。此外，地緣衝突頻傳 ，歐洲國防軍
工仍有投資題材。
策略風格：短期可增加防禦股配置，中長
期看好AI題材如科技及半導體，非AI部分
可關注金融及醫療保健產業
區域：日本銀行股、歐洲金融、歐洲國防
債市
 全球公債殖利率攀升 ，且最新聯準會利率點陣圖顯示 ，官員預估今年底前仍有一碼升息空間 ，立場
偏向維持較高利率水平抑制通膨 發展。殖利率偏高環境建議以中短天期債券投資為主 ，可逢利率走
升時布局進行鎖利領息 。高信評債體質較佳 ，具穩定現金流與還款能力 ，聚焦風險調整後利差具吸

引力者，包含金融、科技、通訊及公用事業等產業。
 可以非美貨幣債券如歐元或澳幣計價投資級債券，分散單一貨幣風險。
債種：中短天期高信評債鎖利，產業如金
融、科技、通訊及公用事業等
衛星配置：非美貨幣債券分散風險
匯市
 聯準會升息且態度偏鷹，美元高檔盤整。
 日本銀行9月升息，但前瞻指引偏中性，未如市場預期的鷹派，日圓震盪。
美元高檔整理，日圓震盪
商品
 中東地緣衝突短期紛擾難解，偏高的油價可能對通膨及實體經濟帶來影響。
 短期受美國雙率走高有壓 ，惟美日財政赤字壓力與去美元化使全球央行買盤依舊 ，提供黃金中長期
穩定支撐。
短期黃金有壓，中長期維持偏多看法
類別 市場看法 偏好資產
策略思維
策略思維 短期抗波動，逢回可分批布局仍具長線趨勢之AI與科技產業

### Page 15

個別客戶因年齡、投資能力、金融商品交易經驗及風險承受度不同，適合之服務及/或金融商品亦有不同，應進一步與專屬理財業務人員進行評估。投資人應徹底瞭解相關服務及金融商品之風險 ，並審慎評估個人財力、資產狀況、
理財需求及風險承受能力，獨立判斷並自行決定是否投資及承擔有關風險。投資任何商品皆具有風險，商品以往之績效不保證未來之投資收益，本公司不負責商品之盈虧，也不保證最低之收益。
本公司保留對本文件及其內容之著作權及其他相關權利，任何人未經本公司同意，不得翻印或作其他任何用途使用(包括但不限於傳送或轉發予其他人士等)。如有違反情事，本公司除不負任何責任外，亦將對未經同意之使用與散布
等行為究責。
基金警語
投資一定有風險，基金投資有賺有賠，申購前應詳閱公開說明書
【信託產品投資須知及風險預告：（請投資人審慎評估下列各項條文內容）】
1. 基金（包括具有定期配息之基金）投資具投資風險，此一風險可能使信託本金發生全部虧損。
2. 基金委託人須自負盈虧，本公司受託投資不保本不保息，除盡善良管理人注意義務外，投資所可能產生的本金虧損、匯率損失、或基金解散、清算、移轉、合併等風險，均由委託人承擔。

3. 本公司受託投資之基金經金管會核准或同意生效，惟不表示絕無風險。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，委託人
申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 投資前應詳閱風險預告及各該基金公開說明書 ，確實作好財務規劃與風險評估 。本公司受託投資之基金已備有公開說明書 (或其中譯本)或投資人須知【前述文件併揭露有關基金應負擔之費用 (境外基金含分銷費用)】，委託人可
至本公司營業處所索取或至公開資訊觀測站、基金資訊觀測站或本公司網站下載。
5. 為保護既有投資人之權益，部份境外基金設有價格稀釋調整機制及公平市價規定，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書及投資人須知。
6. 委託人瞭解並同意受託人辦理特定金錢信託業務(即委託人委託投資基金)交易時，自交易相對人取得之報酬、費用、折讓等各項利益，得作為受託人收取之信託報酬；另委託人在持有基金期間 ，受託人仍持續收受經理費分成報
酬，委託人可至受託人(即本公司)網站 (https://www.kgi.com.tw/zh-tw/)查詢基金之通路報酬。其基金經理費率及其分成費率與其他通路報酬最新變動情形，亦可至該網站查詢。

7. 後收型基金在贖回時，基金公司將依持有期間長短收取不同比率之遞延申購手續費 ，該費用將自贖回總額中扣除；另後收型基金若有收取分銷費 ，則申購手續費雖可遞延收取，惟每年仍需支付0%~1.06%的分銷費（按基金日平
均淨資產價值計算），可能造成實際負擔費用增加（分銷費依各基金公司而有不同，請參考本公司後收型基金投資費用暨特約事項及各基金之投資人須知），另該費用已反映於每日基金淨值中，委託人無需額外支付。
8. 委託人之交易如經基金公司認定為短線交易者，基金公司或本公司可拒絕受理申請轉換或申購要求，並可請求支付一定比例之贖回費用或其他相關費用，費用標準均依各該基金公司之規定。
9. 委託人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績效亦不代表未來績效之保證。基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動，投資人於獲配息時，宜一併注意基
金淨值之變動。又投資新興市場可能比投資已開發國家有較大的價格波動及流動性較低的風險，委託人應慎選投資標的。
10. 部分基金可能持有衍生性商品部位，該部位可能超過基金淨資產價值之100%，並可能因而造成基金淨值高度波動及衍生其他風險。

11. 基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部分，可能導致原始投資金額減損；且部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之費用。由本金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至證券投資信託事業或總代理人之公司
網站查詢。
12. 非投資等級債券基金（本類基金主要係投資於非投資等級之高風險債券且配息來源可能為本金）（主要特色係投資前揭基金之組合基金亦同），適合能承受較高風險之『非保守型』委託人且不宜占投資組合過高的比重。由於非
投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損，不適合無法承擔相關風險之委託人；又如
係本國投信機構募集者，則可能投資於符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，雖其投資總金額不得超過基金淨資產價值之百分之三十，然該債券具有債券發行人違約之信用風險、利率風險、流動性風險、交易對手風險
及因財務訊息揭露不完整或因價格不透明導致波動性較大之風險，投資人應審慎評估；另證券投資信託事業募集之債券型基金、平衡型基金、不動產證券化基金、以追蹤、模擬或複製標的指數表現之指數股票型基金（ETF）及

指數型基金或募集之基金投資新興市場國家債券達基金淨資產價值之百分之六十以上者，或有可能投資符合美國Rule 144A規定具有私募性質之債券，亦應留意相關風險。
13. 投資於中國之基金應遵守中華民國相關法令之規定：境外基金 (不含經金管會核准向證交所申請上市或交易之境外 ETF)如有投資大陸地區之有價證券者，以掛牌上市有價證券及銀行間債券市場為限 ，且投資前述有價證券總金額
不得超過該境外基金淨資產價值之百分之二十(境外基金機構依鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫向金管會申請並經認可者得適用鼓勵境外基金深耕計畫所列放寬前揭投資總金額上限至百分之四十之優惠措施 )；證券投資信託基金雖可投資
大陸地區之有價證券，惟仍應符合信託契約、公開說明書、證券投資信託基金管理辦法等相關規定及受到基金公司QFII額度之限制。又投資人須留意中國市場之政府政策、法令、會計稅務制度、經濟與市場等變動所可能產生之
投資風險。
14. 各基金於公開（含簡式）說明書或投資人須知揭露之風險報酬等級，係依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」而訂定，本公司亦依據投信投顧公會「基金風險報酬等級分類標準」訂定基金產品風險等級。

富管理客服專線或透過財富管理客服信箱服務，查詢申訴案件處理進度。

Document 281: Meta发布8160亿参数“美国最强开放权重模型”，联手苹果让大模型跑上Mac，最强模型仍闭源

Source PDF: all report pdf/Meta发布8160亿参数“美国最强开放权重模型”，联手苹果让大模型跑上Mac，最强模型仍闭源.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-15
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

Muse Spark Open 实际激活参数 420 亿，⽀持 100 万 Token 上下⽂窗⼝，主攻编程与智能体
任务，开发者可通过多个云平台获取，能⼒不及本周⼀推出的 Muse Wonder ；可在两台搭
载苹果 M5 Ultra 芯⽚的 Mac Studio 运⾏。扎克伯格发帖配图称该模型在新 Mac Studio 运⾏
出⾊。 Meta AI 负责⼈称，出于安全性和能⼒两⽅⾯考虑， Meta 通常不会开源最强⼤模
型，但将持续开源优秀模型；免费开放 Muse Spark Open 不会削弱 Meta 在筹建的云业务。
摘要：
Meta 推出开放权重模型 Muse Spark Open ，允许开发者下载模型权重、⾃⾏部署和定制，并与
苹果合作，让模型能够在两台 Mac Studio 上运⾏。与此同时， Meta 仍将能⼒更强的 Muse
Wonder 系列保持闭源，试图在开放开发者⽣态与 AI 商业化之间寻找平衡。
美东时间 15 ⽇周四， Meta 发布号称 “ 迄今美国能⼒最强的开放权重模型 ”Muse Spark Open 。该

模型总参数量达 8160 亿，⽀持 100 万 Token 上下⽂窗⼝，主攻编程与智能体任务，重点提升⻓周
期任务执⾏、复杂软件⼯程和多步推理能⼒。
Meta 当天公布 Muse Spark Open 的模型权重。开发者可以通过多个云平台获取模型，也可以下
载权重，在兼容的硬件和推理框架上⾃⾏运⾏，并使⽤ PyTorch 等⼯具进⼀步定制。模型采⽤
Muse Model License 授权，附带有限商业条款，允许许多开发者免费使⽤。
虽然 Muse Spark Open 的能⼒不及 Meta 本周⼀推出的 Muse Wonder ，但此次发布兑现了 Meta 此
前让其 AI 技术⼴泛向开发者开放的承诺。 Meta 曾凭借 Llama 系列推动开放模型⽣态，在上⼀轮模
型竞争中表现未达预期后，扎克伯格重组 AI 研发体系，转向打造能⼒更强的闭源模型。此次重新
开放模型权重意味着， Meta 正尝试在扩⼤开发者⽣态与保留前沿技术优势之间寻找新的平衡。
8160 亿参数、百万 Token 上下⽂，主攻编程与智能体

Meta 在官⽅博客中表⽰， Muse Spark Open 总参数量为 8160 亿，实际激活参数为 420 亿，拥有
100 万 Token 上下⽂窗⼝，并原⽣⽀持⽂本、图像、视频和⾳频。
与主要⽤于对话和内容⽣成的模型相⽐， Muse Spark Open 更强调⻓周期智能体⼯作。 Meta
称，该模型针对复杂软件⼯程、多步骤推理和需要持续推进的任务进⾏了优化，⽬标是让开发
者构建能够规划、调⽤⼯具并连续执⾏任务的 AI 智能体。
和官⽅博客⼀样， Meta AI 负责⼈、公司⾸席 AI 官 Alexandr Wang 在社交媒体上也将 Muse Spark
Open 称为迄今美国能⼒最强的开放权重模型。他表⽰，该模型专为编码和智能体⼯作设计，例
如⻓时程任务、复杂的软件⼯程、多步骤推理，并转发了 Meta 博客公布的以下该模型多项基准
指标测评成绩与竞品⼤模型对⽐。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

不过， “ 美国最强 ” 仍是 Meta 对⾃⾝产品的评价，并⾮已经获得全⾯独⽴验证的⾏业排名。开放权
重模型的竞争⼒不仅取决于参数规模，还取决于推理效果、运⾏成本、部署难度和开发者实际
使⽤体验。
此次开放的重点是模型权重，⽽⾮意味着所有训练数据、完整训练流程和研发细节均向外界公
开。开发者能够在授权范围内⾃⾏部署和调整模型，但其商业使⽤仍须遵守 Muse Model
License 的相关条款。
Meta 与苹果合作，双 Mac Studio 运⾏⼤模型
此次发布的⼀⼤亮点，是 Meta 与苹果合作，让 Muse Spark Open 能够借助最新 MLX 框架在两台
搭载 M5 Ultra 芯⽚的 Mac Studio 上运⾏。 Meta 官⽅博客称，这是⾸次通过搭载苹果 M5 Ultra 芯⽚
的 Mac Studio 为⽤⼾的桌⾯端带来领先的智能体功能。
扎克伯格在社交媒体发帖称， Muse Spark Open 在新 Mac Studio 上运⾏出⾊，感谢合作⽅苹果
公司及其 CEO 特努斯，并附上⼀张显⽰新模型在 Mac 电脑上运⾏的照⽚。

### Page 3

Meta 表⽰，苹果芯⽚的统⼀内存架构是实现本地部署的关键。每台 M5 Ultra Mac Studio 可提供
最⾼ 512GB 统⼀内存，两台设备协同后能够容纳整个模型。 Meta 称，两台机器可像⼀台拥有约
1TB 统⼀内存的系统⼀样协同⼯作。
媒体认为，这⼀合作为开发者提供了新的选择：他们可以在⾃⼰的硬件上运⾏先进模型，⽽不
必完全依赖云端服务器租⽤算⼒。对于软件开发、研究和视频制作等场景，本地部署也有助于
开发者更直接地控制模型运⾏环境。
Meta 在官⽅博客中还展⽰了模型的本地智能体⼯作流程，包括借助 Muse Code 修改开源 iOS 应
⽤、修复问题并完成构建，以及在 DaVinci Resolve 中逐帧分析视频素材、筛选精彩⽚段并调整
画⾯⽐例。
需要注意的是，模型能够在两台 Mac Studio 上运⾏，并不意味着普通 Mac 电脑也能以相同⽅式运
⾏这⼀完整模型。 Meta 此次展⽰针对的是⾼端硬件配置，本地部署仍⾯临设备成本、内存需求
和推理效率等限制。
对苹果⽽⾔，这⼀合作也提供了展⽰⾼端 Mac 计算能⼒的新场景；对 Meta ⽽⾔，则有助于证明

开放权重模型能够脱离⾃有云端基础设施，在开发者⾃⼰的设备上运⾏。两家公司⻓期以来在
隐私政策、⼴告追踪和计算平台等领域存在竞争，此次合作说明， AI 竞赛正在促使硅⾕企业重新
评估合作边界。
权重免费开放，多平台⽀持降低开发⻔槛
Meta 表⽰， Muse Spark Open 已通过 Baseten 、 Fireworks AI 、 Groq 、 Modal 、 Nebius 、
OpenRouter 和 Together AI 等平台提供 API 服务。模型权重则可从亚⻢逊 SageMaker AI 、⾕歌
Cloud Model Garden 、 Hugging Face 和微软 Foundry Models 等渠道获取。
在 推 理 框 架⽅ ⾯ ， 该 模 型⽀ 持 S G L a n g 、 v L L M 、 Te n s o r RT- L L M 、 H u g g i n g  F a c e
Transformers 、 Apple MLX 、 llama.cpp 和 Unsloth ，并针对 AMD 、苹果、英特尔和英伟达等硬件

平台进⾏了优化。

### Page 4

开发者还可以使⽤ PyTorch 及 TorchTitan 对模型进⾏进⼀步微调，以适应不同应⽤场景。 Meta
称，模型采⽤附带有限商业条款的 Muse Model License ，许多开发者可以免费使⽤。
这套发布⽅式的核⼼，是降低开发者试⽤、部署和定制模型的⻔槛。开发者可以先下载权重，
在本地或⾃⼰的基础设施上开展实验；当需求增⻓、需要更⼤规模的推理能⼒或额外⼯具时，
再选择云端 API 和算⼒服务。
但开放权重并不等于完全没有使⽤限制，也不意味着所有开发者都能零成本运⾏完整模型。硬
件采购、部署运维和推理算⼒仍可能带来可观成本，商业应⽤也需要遵循授权条款。
最强模型仍闭源， Meta 试图兼顾开放⽣态与商业化
Meta 周四透露， Muse Spark Open 的能⼒弱于该司本周⼀推出的 Muse Wonder 。 Meta 选择开放
较低⼀档模型的权重，同时将最先进模型继续保持闭源，反映出公司 AI 战略的变化。
Alexandr Wang 在接受彭博社采访时表⽰： “ 出于安全性和能⼒两⽅⾯的考虑，我们通常不会开

源我们最强⼤的模型。但我们确实认为，随着时间的推移，持续开源优秀的模型⾮常重要，我
们打算继续这样做。 ”
他还表⽰，开放模型对于美国在 AI 竞争中保持优势⾄关重要，并称： “ 世界上⼤多数 AI 都将运⾏
在开放模型上。它们应该是美国的。 ”
这⼀表态也将 Muse Spark Open 放在全球竞争的背景下。阿⾥巴巴、 DeepSeek 等中国企业已在
开放模型领域取得⼴泛关注，开发者能够根据⾃⾝需求调整模型，并将其部署到不同的硬件和
应⽤中。 Meta 希望通过开放权重提供具有竞争⼒的美国替代⽅案，巩固其在全球开发者⽣态中
的影响⼒。
但开放策略也伴随⻛险。模型权重⼀旦⼴泛分发，开发者可以在⼀定程度上⾃⾏调整模型⾏
为，模型提供⽅对最终部署⽅式的控制⼒随之下降。 Meta 表⽰， Muse Spark Open 经过额外安
全训练，并按照公司的安全评估框架进⾏测试；不过，开放模型的安全边界仍取决于后续使⽤
者如何部署和修改模型。
因此， Meta 并未完全回到此前全⾯开放的路线，⽽是采取分层策略：开放权重模型⽤于扩⼤开

发者覆盖⾯，闭源前沿模型则保留技术和商业优势。
免费模型也要变现， Meta 瞄准云端算⼒市场
Muse Spark Open 的另⼀层意义，在于 Meta 希望通过开放模型为⾃⾝云业务和 AI 服务创造需
求。
彭博社报道称， Meta 正在筹建⾃⼰的云业务，计划向客⼾销售 AI 模型访问权限和计算基础设
施。 Alexandr Wang 认为，免费开放 Muse Spark Open 不会削弱这⼀业务，反⽽可以成为吸引
开发者的⼊⼝：⽤⼾先在⾃⼰的设备或基础设施上试⽤模型，随着应⽤规模扩⼤，再为更多算
⼒和⼯具付费。
这⼀模式与 Meta 传统的⼴告业务存在明显区别。开放权重本⾝不直接带来订阅收⼊，但可以扩
⼤模型的使⽤范围，帮助 Meta 建⽴开发者关系，并为后续的云端推理、企业服务和⼯具链创造
商业机会。
与直接向终端⽤⼾销售付费 AI 助⼿相⽐，开放模型的商业回报更依赖⽣态规模和后续服务转化。
开发者是否愿意采⽤ Muse Spark Open ，企业是否愿意为相关云服务付费，都将决定这⼀策略

### Page 5

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

对 Meta ⽽⾔，此次发布既是重返开放模型竞争的信号，也是对 AI 商业模式的⼀次试探。公司希
望通过开放权重扩⼤⽣态影响⼒，同时把最先进模型和⾼附加值服务留在商业体系之内。⾄于
这⼀平衡能否奏效，最终仍取决于模型的实际表现、开发者采⽤率，以及 Meta 能否将开放⽣态

The mood for US Treasuries has calmed.
But don't get too comfortable
Long-end yields have calmed in the past week or so. But pressures

remain. The medium-term issuance narrative stemming from ﬁscal
largesse remains. That’s the 'bad'. Then there’s the 'ugly', in the
guise of energy price pressure stemming from the Iran war. That
has coincided with higher printed inﬂation. Arguably, this is the
biggest direct impact, as it has prompted central bank rate hikes.
And there is the 'good'. Higher real rates have been an important
driver, and a part of this likely incorporates AI-driven productivity

growth. If that last part was the central driver, we’d have little to

The balance of probabilities suggests there is at least one more leg

This could quite easily extend into the ﬁrst quarter of 2027.
For later in 2027, we think inﬂation will come down, and long

story ﬁnally unravelling in a bad way, but we aren't quite
sure we can call for that yet as a baseline view.

slightly above on both counts). That points to 4.75%,

2026.

This is probably overdone, as a swing to the right (should it occur)
could have some tail positives that aren't currently properly
discounted. Look at the improvement seen in Italian spreads
following the rise of Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni. It's been gradual
but persistent, and has not been overly impacted by French
concerns. In that sense, the Italian response has been low beta in
characterisation, which is good.
In fact, the ﬁrst real bout of widening in French spreads can be

traced back to President Emmanuel Macron's election gamble of
mid-2024. Things subsequently calmed, but were not forgotten.
The recent spike is an ampliﬁcation and extension of an underlying
tension that has not gone away. So, overdone? Probably, yes. But
we also remain in a danger zone for bonds generally. Just like in the
US, more overshoots to the upside are entirely foreseeable.

• The global landscape remains complex, shaped by AI, 
energy prices and tariffs. 
• Taiwan’s AI cycle remains strong, with robust AI 
exports and investment and an intact semiconductor 
competitive moat. 
• AI is generating broader domestic spillovers, with 
wealth effects and wage growth supporting 
consumption. 
• Taiwan is cushioning the energy shock by diversifying 
energy imports and absorbing much of the increase in 
upstream costs. 
• AI-driven growth is likely to moderate from 4Q26 into 
2027, as hyperscaler  cash-flow pressures rise and 
higher global rates lift investment costs. 
• Energy remains a n inflation risk, with domestic

November local elections.

in December, amid tighter liquidity conditions.

easing, as excess new -home inventory remains a n

Taiwan: AI, energy and 4Q outlook October 13, 2026

Global backdrop

The global landscape remains complex, 
shaped by the interplay of AI, energy 
prices and tariffs, with significant 
implications for growth, inflation and 
financial conditions.

1) AI

The debate over the durability of the AI 
investment boom continues.  Tailwinds 
include technological advances, broader 
adoption, and s trategic and geopolitical 
considerations. Headwinds range from 
uncertainty over investment returns  and 
hyperscaler financing pressure to 
physical bottlenecks , safety concerns, 
and regulatory uncertainty.

Hyperscaler financing pressure has 
attracted increasing attention . In the 
early stages of the AI investment boom, 
hyperscaler capex was largely financed 
by internal cash flow. This is now 
changing, as free cash flow is expected 
to turn negative from 4Q, requiring major 
players to rely on debt issuance and 
other external financing increasingly.

At the same time, higher global interest 
rates are raising the cost of AI 
investment. The Fed resumed rate hikes 
at its September meeting, while global 
bond yields remain under upward 
pressure amid persistent inflation and 
elevated public and private borro wing 
needs. Our baseline assumes US rates 
will rise by 50bps this year and a further 
25bps in 2027 to 4.50%, with global 
long-term rates also remaining under 
pressure. This could increasingly weigh 
on the economics and pace of AI 
investment.

US-China AI competition is also drawing 
increasing attention.  Across the five 
layers of the AI ecosystem, China is 
making rapid progress in energy, 
infrastructure, AI models and 
applications, supported by its large scale, 
cost-effective domestic manufacturing 
supply chains and huge consumer 
market, which provides a wide range of 
real-world scenarios for AI adoption. The 
emergence of a more integrated and 
cost-competitive Chinese AI ecosystem 
could increasingly challe nge the US 
ecosystem, which currently operates at a 
relatively higher cost.

China, however, remains relatively weak 
in semiconductors.  Around half of 
China's domestic AI chip market still 
relies on foreign suppliers, including 
lower-end NVIDIA chips. This 
semiconductor bottleneck remains a key 
constraint on China's AI development. In 
our view, Taiwanese semiconductor 
companies' competiti ve moat therefore 
remains largely intact in the near term.

to 39% in 2027. Similarly, global 
semiconductor sales remained strong 
through 3Q, while WSTS expects growth 
to moderate from 90% this year to 27 % 
in 2027.

2) Energy

The US -Iran conflict remains at a 
standoff, keeping the energy -price 
outlook highly uncertain.  New 
developments are likely after the US 
midterm elections in November, with 
both further escalation and de -
escalation possible. Crude oil prices have 
remained elevated at around 
USD100/bbl amid a persistent 
geopolitical risk premium . In contrast, 
refined product prices have risen more 
sharply as refining disruptions and 
tighter product logistics have amplified 
the supply shock. Natural gas prices 
have also increased sharply, reflecting 
disruptions to LNG supply and limited 
alternative shipping routes.

Taiwan has been actively diversifying 
its energy import sources to cushion the 
impact. LNG imports from Qatar have 
fallen to zero since April, while imports 
from the US and other suppliers have 
surged to offset the shortfall, helping 
maintain stable domestic electricity 
supply.

3) Tariffs

US tariff actions are likely to accelerate 
after the US midterm elections in 
November. Two major tariff measures 
remain in the pipeline. The Section 301 
forced-labor tariff was implemented in 
July, while final tariff action under the 
Section 301 overcapacity investigation, 
which covers 16 economies, remains 
pending. Meanwhile, Phase 1 of the 
Section 232 semiconductor tariffs was 
implemented in January, while Phase 2, 
covering a broader range of 
semiconductor-related products, 
remains pending.

Taiwan has secured a relatively 
favourable framework through its trade 
and investment agreements with the 
US. Taiwan signed an Agreement on 
Reciprocal Trade (ART) and an 
Investment MOU with the US earlier this 
year. The ART establishes a 15% US tariff 
rate on Taiwanese goods, while the MOU 
provides Taiwan with preferential 
treatment under Section 232, including 
for semiconductors. The ART has yet to 
enter into force, and it remains to be 
seen how the final Section 301 tariff 
burden will interact with the 15% ART 
framework. Meanwhile, major Taiwanese 
companies such as TSMC are expanding 
their US investments under the 
Investment MOU, which could support 
relatively favourable treatment for 
Taiwan when broader Section 232 
semiconductor tariffs are introduced.

Taiwan 4Q outlook

Taiwan has navigated the complex 
global environment relatively well so far 
this year, supported by robust AI exports 
and investment, diversified energy 
imports, and efforts to absorb higher 
upstream energy costs. Some 
normalisation is likely in 4Q26 –2027, as 
the AI -driven growth boost moderates 
and domestic energy prices face greater 
pressure to adjust.

1) Growth

GDP growth remains broadly on track 
with our expectations. High -frequency 
indicators suggest that GDP growth 
should remain around 12% yoy in 3Q, 
broadly in line with 12.9% in 2Q but below 
the 15.4% peak in 1Q. Growth is expected 
to moderate to a high -single-digit pace 
from 4Q onwards. We maintain our full -
year GDP growth forecasts at 11.6% for 
2026 and 5.6% for 2027.

The AI cycle remains the key driver of 
Taiwan's growth.  Information and 
communication products and electronic 
components accounted for nearly 80% 
of total exports in the first nine months 
of this year, while manufacturing 
accounts for nearly 50% of gross fixed

capital formation. From 4Q onwards, 
export growth is expected to moderate 
from exceptionally strong levels to a 
more normal but still robust pace, 
broadly following the trajectory of 
global hyperscaler capex and 
semiconductor sales. Investment is likely 
to follow a similar path.

For now, Taiwanese technology 
companies continue to report robust 
revenue growth, led by semiconductors, 
ODMs/EMS, and PCs and servers. 
Capacity utilisation at leading Taiwanese 
foundries has also continued to rise, 
suggesting that investment demand 
remains strong.

The AI boom is also beginning to 
generate broader spillovers to the 
domestic economy. The AI-driven equity 
rally has generated significant wealth 
effects, with the TAIEX up 70% year-to-
date and Taiwan becoming the world's 
fifth-largest stock market. Taiwanese 
households have benefited from the 
appreciation of financial assets, with 
equities and mutual funds accounting for 
nearly 30% of total household financial 
assets. The equity rally has also 
generated positive income effects. Wage 
growth has picked up, led by the services 
sector, particularly financial services, as 
strong earnings at financial 
institutions—especially securities 
firms—have prompted higher 
compensation to retain talent. These 
wealth and income gains should support 
household consumption, creating a 
positive feedback loop that broadens the 
benefits of the AI boom across the wider 
economy.

                                                                                                                                            7

2) Inflation

CPI inflation is also tracking broadly in 
line with our forecast. Headline CPI 
remained elevated at 2.4% yoy in 3Q, up 
from 1.2% in 1Q and 2.2% in 2Q. We 
maintain our forecast for headline CPI 
inflation to remain close to 2.5% in 4Q, 
and retain our full-year forecasts at 2.0% 
for 2026 and 2.1% for 2027.

Energy inflation remains the main 
upside risk.  The government has 
absorbed much of the increase in energy 
costs through state -owned CPC and 
Taipower, limiting the pass -through to 
domestic consumers. With losses at both 
companies widening, the government 
will need to decide whether to provide 
additional subsidies to cover the losses 
or allow further increases in domestic 
energy prices. The September re view 
postponed the adjustment to the 
December meeting, when the overall 
situation will be reassessed after the 
November local elections. Taipower's  
latest review indicated that electricity 
rates would need to rise by 12.8%. A hike 
in electricity prices could lift inflation 
expectations and generate second -
round effects, particularly as the labour 
market tightens and wage pressures 
increase.

3) Monetary policy

The central bank kept the policy 
discount rate unchanged at 2.00% at its 
September meeting, in line with our 
expectations. We continue to expect a 
modest 12.5bp rate hike at the 
December meeting, bringing the policy 
rate to 2.125%.  By then, CPI inflation is 
likely to have remained above 2% for 
around six consecutive months. The 
possibility of an increase in electricity 
prices following the November local 
elections could further lift inflation 
expectations, strengthening the case for 
a December rate hike.

Financial conditions have already 
tightened despite the absence of a 
policy rate hike.  M2 growth remained 
around 7% yoy in 3Q, well below double-
digit nominal GDP growth. At the same 
time, bank loan growth has accelerated 
to more than 10% yoy, driven by strong

                                                                                                                                            8

corporate credit demand, particularly AI-
related fundraising by technology 
companies. Tighter financial conditions 
are also evident in rising market interest 
rates, including commercial paper rates 
and government bond yields.

The central bank has continued to ease 
housing credit controls gradually. At its 
September meeting, it raised the LTV 
cap on second -home mortgage loans to 
70%, the second easing of housing credit 
controls this year. There is scope for 
further easing, including removing the 
LTV cap on second-home loans or raising 
the LTV caps for third - and fourth-home 
loans and high -value properties, which 
currently stand at 30%. However, the 
central bank is likely to proceed 
cautiously, given concerns that a faster 
relaxation could reignite  speculative 
demand and put renewed upward 
pressure on housing prices.

4) Housing

A gradual and modest recovery in the 
housing market is expected from 4Q26 
into 2027.  The existing -home market 
has already shown early signs of 
recovery, with Sinyi  housing prices 
returning to slightly positive yoy growth 
since 2Q. Total building transactions also 
returned to positive yoy growth in 3Q. 
The easing of credit controls should help 
reinforce this modest recovery trend.

The new -home market remains under 
pressure. The number of new units under 
construction has started to decline since 
2023, while new completions are  only 
beginning to ease this year . Demand 
remains weak, as potential buyers 
continue to wait for lower prices. Unsold 
new homes stood at 113K units at end -
2025, compared with a historical normal 
level of around 80K, implying excess 
inventory of roughly 30K units. At the 
current pace of new -home transactions, 
it would take roughly one year to 
normalize the existing excess inventory, 
and longer when factoring in the new 
supply pipeline. Some industry experts 
estimate that it could take as long as 3.8 
years digest current inventories fully 
(here).

### Page 2

The Week Ahead October 2, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         2

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

India: RBI monetary policy committee 
meeting (7-Oct)

The macro environment has undergone 
significant changes since the RBI 
monetary policy committee’s (MPC) 
August policy meeting, with global oil 
prices rising past $100bl, and US Fed 
kickstarting their rate hike cycle. 
Domestically, core and headline 
continued to rise, laying the ground for 
inflation to quicken beyond 5.8 -6.0% in 
the second half as price risks broaden. 
Strong 1QFY27 GDP growth meanwhile 
pointed to the economy’s relative 
resilience to external shocks.

Besides fuel feeding into food costs, 
weather has also proved to be disruptive, 
with indications that the El Niño 
phenomenon could extend into 2027. 
International food benchmarks, including 
the UN Food Price index and the 
Bloomberg spot agricultural indice s, 
continue to climb. India’s food inflation 
has become more broad -based, shifting 
beyond vegetables and pulses to sugar, 
milk, protein groups, and edible oils. Sub-
par weather has contributed to this 
upmove, besides idiosyncratic factors 
like swing in production trends (cobweb), 
diversion for fuel usage etc.

After two years of normal monsoon, 
cumulative southwest rainfall this year is 
set to end with rainfall around 12 -13% 
below the long -term average, the most 
since 2015 and resulting in a slower 
build-up in reservoir levels.  
Global benchmark oil price gauge has 
averaged ~$93pb in FY27 year -to-date, 
up 36% yoy  vs FY26. Retail pump fuel 
prices were adjusted in quick succession 
in May26 and have been left unchanged 
since then, while other fuel products 
including CNG, aviation fuel, LPG etc 
have continued to climb. Add to this, 
other pockets like telecom, restaura nts, 
business services, transport fares etc 
have been inching up, reflecting early 
signs of broadening in price pressures (on 
sequential basis). A gradual broadening 
of price pressures is likely to keep 
headline inflation high and narrow the 
real rate buffer significantly.

Authorities are also conducting liquidity 
absorption measures through market 
operations like VRRR, open market 
operations and sell-buy FX swaps. This is 
likely to align the overnight call money 
rates to the benchmark repo rate, after 
having fallen sharply d ue to the surplus 
in the banking system.  Liquidity and core 
inflation have historically displayed a 
modest positive relationship, as per our 
study. As such, the RBI's inflation 
mandate has likely reinforced the push to 
drain excess liquidity.

We expect the RBI MPC to raise the repo 
rate by 25bp to 5.5% , citing pipeline 
inflationary pressures. Stance could be 
changed to withdrawal of 
accommodation to signal hawkish intent. 
We expect one more hike within 4Q26, 
though the odds of additional hikes have 
risen as oil prices stay stubbornly high 
and weather remains unfavourable.

Malaysia

We expect industrial production to grow 
by 5.8% yoy in August from 4.7% yoy in 
July. Strength in  export-oriented 
manufacturing is likely to persist , 
supported by sustained demand for 
artificial intelligence-related electronics 
goods, while mining output may have 
recovered from its contraction in July.

Taiwan

September trade and inflation data are 
forthcoming. Export growth is expected 
to sustain the 40% pace seen in the 
previous month. The global AI boom is 
facing rising headwinds from 
synchronised rate hikes by G3 central 
banks, higher bond yields, and rising  
financing costs. That said, there are so 
far no clear signs of a significant 
slowdown, based on the high -frequency 
indicators we track across AI demand, 
investment, and supply.

CPI is expected to rebound to 2.4% YoY 
in September, up from 2.0% in August. 
The earlier arrival of the Mid -Autumn 
Festival this year may have boosted food 
and services prices on a YoY basis. The 
rebound in global energy prices should 
also have lifted tran sport costs, 
including gasoline prices and airfares. 
While electricity tariffs will remain 
frozen, the December review implies a 
possibility of an electricity price hike 
after the local elections.

Given still -strong economic growth, 
persistent inflation, and the potential for 
inflation expectations to rise, we 
maintain our view that the central bank 
will raise the policy rate by a modest 
12.5bps to 2.125% in December.

Thailand

We expect Thailand’s headline inflation 
to accelerate to 3.2% yoy in September 
from 2.5% yoy  in August, slightly 
exceeding the central bank’s 1 -3% 
inflation target. Faster increases in 
energy and food prices likely pushed 
overall inflation higher, despite an 
easing in electricity tariffs starting 
September. We expect the Bank of 
Thailand (BOT), w hich views the rise in 
inflation as largely supply -driven, to 
keep its policy rate stable at 1.00% for 
the remainder of 2026, aiming to support 
an economy with low and uneven growth.

Report Introduction
Our proprietary dbDataInsights survey shows cyber threats are evolving faster than organisations can adapt
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research.
“It’s the only thing that can shut down my business.”
That was the punchline from a conversation I had recently with the CEO of a large
privately held financial services company. This was his comment when I followed up

as to why cybersecurity risk is the number one concern that “keeps him up at night”.
To help understand this risk, our dbDataInsights team surveyed more than 50
cybersecurity experts. The uncomfortable conclusion is that many firms are
unprepared for cyber threats. Cyber risk is increasing as AI adoption accelerates.
—89% agree the threat landscape is evolving faster than organisations can adapt.
—78% say the gap between highly and poorly prepared organisations is widening.

What has changed with AI advancement? Cyberattacks have become faster,
cheaper, more deceptive and more scalable. Capabilities once reserved for
sophisticated actors are now broadly accessible to a new generation of profit-driven
criminals. At the same time, rising geopolitical tension has made cyber operations
an increasingly attractive low-cost, deniable tool for nation states. Corporates and
investors therefore need to understand their broader exposure and critical

infrastructure risk in their supply chain and with stakeholders.
Among other autonomous incidents, the recent Hugging Face incident
highlighted AI’s cyberattack potential. AI agents caused harm without
direction from a human attacker, with weak guardrails and no human
judgement to restrain them. Beyond the systemic questions it raised, the attack
highlights three additional concerns: (i) AI operates at a speed and scale humans
cannot match, with agents taking over 17,000+ actions in four days; (ii) the intrusion

began in the data-processing pipeline, where AI platforms are uniquely exposed;
and (iii) the agents escaped their test environment through a widely used third-party
tool representing supplier risk in an unfamiliar form.

Report summary and notable takeaways
Secular trends and AI adoption have widened the attack surface; the severity of losses is compounding
Source: FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3), company security advisories, OECD, company reports, Deutsche Bank Research.
Secular trends have widened the attack surface. This includes rapid
digitalisation, widespread Internet of Things (IoT) endpoints, hybrid workforces,
cloud adoption and deeper supply chain dependencies. The scale of the threat

increase is reflected in data from the FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3),
which shows cybercrime complaints more than doubled from 2019 pre-pandemic
baseline of ~467,000 to ~1.0mn in 2025, with a 3x expansion relative to 2017 levels.
Barriers to entry are lower; attack tools are now sold “off the shelf”. Today,
cybercrime marketplaces, available on the dark web, look less like the underworld
portrayed in films and more like an online software store. Buyers can access AI-

powered tools that scan for software vulnerabilities (see top-right chart showing
reported CVEs after the release of cyber capable frontier models), write malicious
code at speed, and provide access to malware that rewrites its own code to avoid
detection as it runs. Ransomware-as-a-service (RaaS) takes it further, renting out
ready-made attack kits, often in exchange for a portion of the profits.
Impersonation is becoming harder to detect. Hackers can now generate more

realistic phishing messages, while voice cloning and deepfakes make impersonation
convincing enough to gain access unnoticed. With stolen credentials, attackers often
aren’t hacking in, they are simply logging in.
The rise of loose cybercriminal collectives. Some of the most damaging recent
attacks have been linked to loosely organised, often English-speaking networks such
as ShinyHunters and Scattered Spider. The latter has been widely named as the

group behind the 2025 Marks & Spencer cyberattack, in which the hacking group
deployed RaaS. AI is enabling these groups to become larger and faster, widening
the gap between the speed of attack and a defender’s response.
The severity of losses is compounding. A 2026 IBM study puts the average breach
at a record USD ~5.0mn, up 12% YoY, but the impact on large companies can run
into hundreds of millions or even billions. Estimates for global cyber insurance

premiums (per Munich Re) point to a rise from roughly USD 7.0bn in 2020 to USD
15.0bn in 2025, with expectations to reach USD 28.0bn by 2030.

### Page 5

5
Among the more concerning survey takeaways is that a large-scale and
significant systemic incident is now the base case.
— 83% expect a significant systemic attack within two years and 81% expect it to be
moderately, very, or extremely severe for economic activity and markets.
— 81% say AI favours attackers today, with not one respondent putting defenders
ahead of attackers. Only 20% expect defenders to be ahead in five years.
Geopolitical tensions coupled with rising cyber capabilities have transformed

cyberspace into a digital battlefield between global powers. Intent has moved
towards espionage and disruption of not only critical infrastructure (e.g., healthcare,
finance, power, industrial systems, defence) but private companies as well. Chinese
President Xi Jinping has linked cybersecurity to national security and economic and
social stability, with China’s public statements similarly emphasising the protection
of critical infrastructure. At the same time, the US’s June 2025 Executive Order on

Cybersecurity reflects its continued focus on national cyber resilience.
Europe relies on US cyber tools while its start-ups lag in funding. Reliance on US
security vendors exposes European firms to potential access, update or export
restrictions over the long-term. Shrinking this gap will prove difficult given US cyber
start-ups attract nearly 6x the venture and growth funding of European peers. And
while the timing of quantum computing (which could break widely used encryption

methods) remains uncertain, it could further deepen Europe’s dependency.
Ultimately, cyber preparedness is a financial variable. Investors should ask what
firms spend on security versus AI adoption, how well they know their vulnerabilities,
and how fast they can recover.
— Only 24% of respondents think organisations have sufficient visibility into the
threats relevant to them, even though 57% saw cybersecurity budgets rise over
the past 12 months.

— 81% agree that cybersecurity related laws and regulation cannot keep pace, and
65% doubt leadership understands cyber risk well enough.

think a systemic cyber incident is likely

### Page 6

6
When cyber weakness = share price consequence…
Investors are more aware of cyber risk today, but they rarely sell a stock due to
perceived cyber vulnerabilities (although we are aware of some who have).
—What is the potential impact? Our dbDataInsights event study of 47 breaches
shows a cumulative breach-attributed return of c. -2.9% within the first two
trading days and c. -6.0% through first 20 days trading days. The erosion then
continues more gradually, reaching c. -6.7% nine months after disclosure (see

slide 13).
Which industries are most vulnerable?
—Our survey respondents point to (1) government & public sector (52%), (2)
financial services (50%), (3) energy and utilities (46%) and (4) healthcare and life
sciences follow (39%, rising to 48% among US respondents).
We should caveat, that high sector exposure does not mean every company in it is
equally vulnerable. Some firms in these “exposed” sectors may very well be better
prepared given they have built specialist teams and defence protocols for decades.

What about access to credit and ratings? Credit and rating providers are paying
attention. Moody’s and S&P both cut their outlook on Jaguar Land Rover within two
months of its 2025 hack. Today, this type of impact is not widespread. But if risk
escalates through a systemic incident or an AI-driven step change in attacks these
mechanisms, including ESG ratings, could reprice asset ownership or impact access
to credit. In fact, some lenders are already pricing it. Journal of Corporate Finance

research using US syndicated loan data found borrowers with persistently higher cyber
risk pay 4–13bp more.
The bottom line? While a broader systemic attack is hard to prepare for, corporates
and investors should be asking questions about vulnerabilities, security investment,
preparedness and recovery should an attack occur. This is especially true for
industries quickly adopting AI capabilities and / or a high threat landscape.

dbDataInsights Cybersecurity Expert Survey methodology
—This survey was conducted among 54 cybersecurity professionals across the US
and Europe in September of 2026. Questions took the form of either multiple

choice selections or free form responses. The dbDataInsights team received the
responses anonymously, but respondents were required to work or consult in
cyber security roles
—Who took the survey? On self-identified roles, around 80% sit inside an
organisation’s cybersecurity function, with the remainder working independently
(15%) or in academia and research (6%). The most common titles were security
architect (23%), Chief Information Security Officer (16%) and security analyst (9%),

alongside Head of Security, Deputy CISO, Incident Response Lead and Security
Operations Lead.
—Only a small minority of respondents work for cyber resellers, thus survey views
are primarily attributable to those working within an organization on
cybersecurity.
—Survey participants were also asked for their perspectives on cyber resilience and
governance, what is misunderstood, alongside emerging threats, risk to AI
adoption and the influence of geopolitics.

—While responses were anonymous, in some cases we conducted follow up
interviews with certain participants where contact details were made available.
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research.

### Page 10

Risk from AI-enabled attacks and added AI adoption vulnerabilities
Insider threats and supply chain compromises remain a growing concern
“The fear of AI writing undetectable malware is … overblown. Attackers were
never short of malware, since they can buy it ready made along with initial
access. Good detection looks at behaviour rather than exact code, so a rewritten
payload still has to do the same suspicious things.”
“Frontier AI models are currently the main hype. Automated phishing and

scams are underappreciated, yet they are responsible for the bigger
monetary losses at the moment.”
“There are too many organisations relying on the same providers for
critical services and this means single points of failure.”
“A corporation might be very secure but if they rely on a third party that
does not have the same level of security posture, [it] could be
compromised via this third-party (basically their weak link).”
“Existing employee login behaviour and usage of AI is underappreciated –

it’s what poses the biggest threat to a breach.”
“No company can guarantee they won’t be breached, so there needs to
be more focus put on the ability to recover and survive an attack.”
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research .

### Page 11

11
Experts believe governments are not keeping pace, and no one owns AI oversight
How experts surveyed by dbDataInsights view governance and regulation
—AI adoption is outpacing internal controls. As one expert put it: “Companies
are pushing the use of AI more to their employees without the proper
mechanisms and policies in place to prevent misuse or attacks initiated by the AI
systems themselves.”
—Regulation is struggling to keep pace. 81% of respondents agree that

cybersecurity-related laws and regulations are failing to keep up with
technological change, and 54% say regulatory requirements can improve security
but may also slow innovation. One respondent highlighted “the levels of
uncertainty, the long running nature of the problems, and the rapidity of change.”
—Responsibility for AI oversight remains unsettled. International bodies are the
leading choice, supported by 50% of respondents. No option commands a

majority, underscoring the lack of consensus on who should oversee AI
development.
—Better board questions may not guarantee better decisions. The survey
points to more informed questioning by corporate leadership, yet 65% doubt that
leadership teams understand cyber risk well enough to make informed business
trade-offs. Measuring activity more effectively than outcomes further complicates
oversight.
—Accountability is becoming personal. Only 43% say accountability for cyber risk

is clearly defined in their governance framework, yet the consequences now reach
the top as a handful of CEOs have left or gone into early retirement after
financially consequential cyber breaches.
Who should primarily oversee AI development?

Material cyber breaches can cause a notable drop in share price
Our dbDataInsights study found a 2.9% drop on average within two trading days, with the cumulative breach-attributed return of -6.0% on trading day 20.

Source: dbDataInsights, Bloomberg Finance LP., Deutsche Bank Research, Company filings. Event study of reported cybersecurity incidents, 2015 -2025, 34 US and 13 European companies across nine sectors.
What typically happens to a company’s shares when a breach is reported?
—Short-term impact: The market prices the breach immediately. Our model shows
cumulative breach-attributed (i.e. isolating only the part of the share price move
we can pin on the breach itself) returns are c. -2.9% within two trading days of

at one year, although the one-year point estimate (our single best-guess figure)
should be treated with caution given the width of the confidence band.
—Pre-breach price behaviour: We find no evidence of information leakage ahead
of disclosure. While the breach component for the [-10,-1] window (ten trading
days before the news broke) is +0.04%, the corresponding t-stat value of 0.03 falls
notably below the usual bar for significance (c.2). As such, we conclude the market

had not begun reacting before the announcement.
Event study methodology
—Sample: 47 publicly reported cybersecurity incidents across the US and Europe
over 2015-2025, measured against each company’s regional sector benchmark.
Requirement of 5 years of stock returns pre-attack and 1 year of post-attack.
—Day 0: the day an incident was first publicly reported or disclosed.
—Baseline: We use five years of pre-incident share-price performance to
estimate: (i) Beta (β): how sensitive a company’s returns are to movements in its

sector and (ii) Alpha (α): the extent to which the company typically outperforms
or underperforms its sector after adjusting for that sensitivity.
—Attribution: post-incident returns are split into sector-relative returns, alpha
(α) and breach (abnormal returns attributed to the disclosure).
—95% confidence interval: Range within which we can say with 95% confidence
that the real share price impact lies, based on our sample data.
Total return attribution

Marks & Spencer (Reported Apr 2025)
What happened?
A breach via an attack on a connected 3rd
party forced the suspension of all online
orders and led to empty store shelves.
What did it cost?
~£136mn of direct costs related to the
attack, estimated group operating profit
impact of around £300mn for the FY. M&S
also said they had received £100mn via
cyber insurance.
Coupang (Reported Nov 2025)
What happened?
A data breach exposed the records of
more than 30mn users with a former

employee reportedly behind the attack.
What did it cost?
The firm was fined more than ~625bn won
($409mn) (2/3rd directly attributable to
data leak), and also announced a $1.2bn
voucher compensation scheme.
UnitedHealth (Reported Feb 2024)
What happened?
Hackers reportedly used stolen credentials
to access a subsidiary’s (Change
Healthcare) systems, stealing data and
deploying ransomware.
What did it cost?
Total costs exceeded $3bn, with the CEO
summoned to testify before Congress,

where he confirmed that a $22mn ransom
was paid.
Equifax (Reported Sep 2017)
What happened?
A data breach saw hackers expose
sensitive customer information, including
145.5mn Social Security numbers and
more than 200k credit card numbers.
Attackers reportedly moved inside Equifax
systems for 76 days before being detected.
What did it cost?
Settlements, fines and legal costs
exceeded $1bn, while the firm also spent
more than $1.5bn on tech upgrades

following the incident.

Notable cyber attacks: how they happened, estimated costs and share price reaction, cont.
Nike (Reported Jan 2026)
What happened?
A cybercrime group leaked what it claimed
was 1.4TB of internal Nike data (appearing
to cover metrics such as product
specifications and pricing), following expiry
of a ransom deadline.
What did it cost?
While Nike did confirm it was investigating
the incident, it did not report any
operational disruption. Underperformance

shown in the chart does not appear to be
related to the breach.
T-Mobile (Reported Aug 2021)
What happened?
An attacker reportedly targeted an
exposed router as an entry point, before
using brute force attacks to gain further
access to T-Mobile systems.
What did it cost?
In 2022 the firm agreed to pay $500mn to
settle a class-action lawsuit ($350mn
payment to class members  plus $150mn
on security spend) but has since suffered
further data breaches, contributing to an

additional $31.5mn regulatory settlement.
Coinbase (Reported May 2025)
What happened?
Attackers bribed at least one support
agent at a connected 3rd party to access
Coinbase support systems and steal
customer data, then tricked users into
sending crypto.
What did it cost?
Coinbase refused to pay the $20mn
ransom demanded by the attackers to
keep the attack quiet. Instead they
reimbursed affected users and cited costs
of up to $400mn. The stock impact was

short-lived, though MSCI applied an ESG
score deduction.
Jaguar Land Rover (Reported Sep 2025)
What happened?
Production was shut down for five weeks
due to what was deemed a ransomware
attack; though no ransom was asked for
(later reporting suggested potential Russia
links were being investigated).
What did it cost?
JLR posted £485mn Q2’ FY26 loss (vs.
£398mn profit in Q2’ FY25), with an
additional £196mn in “cyber-related costs”.
These impacts filtered through to the

share price of JLR’s Parent (Tata Motors),
while UK economic impacts were
estimated at £1.9bn.

### Page 16

16
Targets go beyond tech and can also impact critical infrastructure
MGM Resorts (Reported Sep 2023)
What happened?
A vishing call to the IT help desk was reportedly used to reset credentials, after which
ransomware took down hotel systems, bookings and casino floor operations for several
days.
What did it cost?
MGM declined to pay a ransom. Around $100mn hit to third-quarter 2023 results plus
roughly ~$10mn of additional one-time expenses related to the incident.

Caesars Entertainment (Reported Sep 2023)
What happened?
The same social-engineering group as in the MGM incident targeted an outsourced IT
support vendor, gaining access to the loyalty programme database and member data.
What did it cost?
Caesars paid roughly $15mn of a reported $30mn demand and kept customer-facing
operations running.
NHS / Synnovis (Reported Jun 2024)
What happened?
Ransomware against pathology provider Synnovis significantly reduced its capacity to

process tests leading to delays / cancellations of over 11,000 appointments.
What did it cost?
Estimated 2024 direct operational financial impact of around GBP 32mn for Synnovis,
several times the profit reported in the prior year, plus wider NHS disruption costs.
Colonial Pipeline (Reported May 2021)
What happened?
A compromised VPN credential without multi-factor authentication allowed attackers to
access the network where they subsequently deployed ransomware. The operator shut the

pipeline down, triggering fuel shortages (c. 70% of gas stations in some states ran out of
fuel) across the US East Coast.
What did it cost?
A $4.4mn ransom was paid, of which about $2.3mn was later recovered by the US
authorities.
Bitget (Reported Sep 2026)
What happened?
Attackers exploited a vulnerability in a 3rd party security product to steal internal network
credentials, forge withdrawal instructions, bypass risk controls and ultimately steal crypto.

What did it cost?
An estimated $388mn worth of funds was transferred out by the attackers. While the firm
reimbursed affected users via its User Protection Fund, there were significant outflows
following the incident as users moved to take funds off the platform.
Progress Software (Reported May 2023)
What happened?
Attackers exploited a zero-day SQL injection vulnerability in Progress Software’s MOVEit file
transfer system. This was then used by attackers to steal data from firms running the

Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026

### Page 17

17
Cyber breaches can impact consumer behaviour
Roughly 4 in 10 consumers we surveyed say they would stop buying from a company they shop with after a known breach or attack.
Source: dbDataInsights monthly consumer survey, Deutsche Bank Research. EU Average includes France, Germany, Italy and Spain.
How will customers respond to a data breach once it occurs?
dbDataInsights survey of consumer responses, % of respondents
How will customers react once the breach is resolved?

Consumer responses among those who would either stop buying or were unsure, % of respondents
Consumer surveys suggest that cyber breaches can undermine customer
loyalty.  Results of our dbDataInsights Consumer survey show the potential
mindset of a consumer after a cyber breach occurs:
—Across the US, UK and EU average, 39–41% say they would stop buying, while only
27–28% would continue. Around a third are unsure.
—Among those who would stop buying or are unsure, only 18–22% say they would

return as normal. Another 30–35% would buy selected products only, 17–20%
would not return, and 28–29% remain unsure. Note that this implies a range of
13-15% believe they would not shop at the store again after the breach is
resolved.
Admittedly, these stated intentions may overstate actual switching, particularly for
essential purchases where alternatives are limited. Nevertheless, the findings
suggest that cyber breaches that put customer privacy at risk can influence

purchasing decisions, especially if they have alternatives.

dbDataInsights Consumer Survey Methodology
—Our proprietary survey conducted in 2026 (January – September) asked

consumers how they would respond if a company they shopped at regularly
was involved in a data breach or cyberattack, and how they would respond
once the disruption was resolved. This monthly survey covers households and
consumers in Germany, France, Italy, Spain, the United Kingdom, and the
United States.
—For each country referenced above, a nationally representative sample of more
than 500 households is surveyed each month (over 600 households in the US).

Collectively, this meant over the course of the survey (January – September
2026), we surveyed almost 30,000 households.

### Page 18

18
Material cyber incidents have contributed to negative credit outlooks
The infrastructure exists for cyberattacks to have a greater impact on credit quality should cyber disruption continue to accelerate
Source: S&P, Moody’s, Fitch, Deutsche Bank Research.
—Credit rating agencies consider both pre-breach resilience frameworks and
post-breach financial impacts. Agencies assess pre-breach cyber preparedness
through a lens of mitigating risk, while post-breach credit impacts centre on

financial capacity to withstand the resulting shock (considers scale and length of
the disruption); highly leveraged issuers with limited liquidity are seen as more
vulnerable. Negative outlook changes at JLR and Equifax illustrate the potential
credit impact. Fitch generally considers permanent reductions in cash generation
more credit-significant than temporary cash-flow delays.
—Ratings agencies are moving towards continuous monitoring. In 2021,

Moody’s invested $250mn in BitSight, which produces daily security ratings.
Moody’s has since built BitSight’s ratings into its credit tools, including Credit
Catalyst and EDF-X, for uses such as underwriting and ongoing monitoring of
borrowers and suppliers. This means lenders can see a company’s exposed
vulnerabilities as a potential indicator of corporate cyber risk.
S&P Global:
“The increasing frequency of cyberattacks and the potential for rapid deterioration in credit

profiles after an attack are risk factors relevant for our rating assessments.”
Moody’s:
“Cyberattacks can undermine the creditworthiness of companies, organizations and
governments, across all sectors and geographies, hitting revenues, increasing costs, and eroding
customer trust.”
Fitch Ratings:
“Although cyber events cannot be predicted with great accuracy, the associated risk can be
managed through proactive strategies, technology, policies, and vigilance.”

“The chief executive officer and board of directors hold ultimate responsibility for managing
cyber risk.”
Example rating actions where cyber was a named factor
Outlook changes due to operational disruption, financial cost and reporting delays

Cyber governance & breaches can impact ESG scores
Sectors that hold the most personal data, and monetise it most directly, carry the greatest risk weighting in mainstream ESG scores
Source: MSCI ESG Ratings Methodology, Morningstar Sustainalytics ESG Risk Ratings, SASB Materiality Map, Deutsche Bank Resear ch.
Cybersecurity is generally seen as both a social and governance consideration
MSCI and Morningstar Sustainalytics both assess data privacy and security through management of the

risk, exposure to it and the controversies that follow a breach. Weak governance weighs on the score,
and a breach can cut it directly – more severe controversies draw larger deductions that persist for
longer.
Materiality varies sharply by sector. SASB flags data security as material across most consumer,
financial, healthcare and technology industries, with customer privacy concentrated in the most data-
intensive ones (see right).

### Page 20

20
IV. Investment, risk and opportunity

### Page 21

21
Europe’s dependency on US tech is firmly in the spotlight
Access to frontier AI models is now a security question; the funding gap for Europe will be hard to close
Source: Tikehau Capital – Tikehau Capital Barometer of European Investment in Cybersecurity – 7th edition 2026, Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank Research.
—Growing cyber risk creates increased dependency on US cyber tech. Growing
concern over the cyber capabilities of frontier AI models has made ensuring

access to such technology a national security priority. US dominance over the AI
tech chain (e.g. chips, model development, etc), its willingness to impose AI-
related controls / restrictions, and previous pressure against Europe over tech
regulations has amplified concerns that dependence on US tech represents a
strategic threat to European sovereignty.
—Funding is the structural challenge. US cybersecurity start-ups attract almost 6x
the funding of European peers, and US-domiciled firms dominate the major

indices – 89% of the Nasdaq CTA Cybersecurity index and 93% of Global X
Cybersecurity ETF (which seeks to track the performance of Indxx Cybersecurity
Index) by weight. Europe has also seen home-grown firms such as Darktrace
acquired by US companies, though this reflects broader market trends rather
than anything cyber-specific. Ultimately, the US has a greater exit strategy for
start-ups as well.
—Boosting homegrown solutions a focus, but also a risk. EU policymakers are

seeking to address the dependency; e.g. the EU’s Cybersecurity and AI Action Plan
includes “scaling up Europe’s AI capabilities for cybersecurity” as one of its three
objectives. However, there are also risks associated with this endeavour; as one
dbDataInsights survey participant put it, “for most European organisations, the
bigger near-term danger would be moving too fast to less capable providers.”
—The wider relationship adds to the exposure. A more transactional

international environment, alongside Europe’s dependency on the US for energy
supplies and defence support, compounds the risk. Recent US-EU diesel
negotiations serve as a pertinent example of this, whereby the US reportedly
considered a diesel export ban to compel a release of European diesel reserves.

Cybersecurity as an opportunity
Tailwinds include demand related to keeping operations secure with AI acceleration and consolidation tailwinds
Source: dbDataInsights, Deutsche Bank Research. Investors, defined as those who invest in stocks/ shares (either individually or through a broker/advisor) on a regular basis , were asked whether they perceived cybersecurity / data privacy as a risk or an opportunity for investors in 2026.
Broadly, DB’s company research view is that cybersecurity becomes even

more important in the AI era. Whereas cybersecurity has historically been viewed
as a blocker/drag on innovation and operational speed, it is now viewed as a
strategic enabler for secure AI adoption.
In particular, network security may see renewed growth after being viewed as a
more mature category. Rising AI usage should drive additional compute and data-
centre capacity, expanding the infrastructure requiring segmentation and
protection. Agentic workloads may also generate lower-throughput but more

frequent and persistent interactions across models, tools and data sources,
increasing the volume and complexity of network-level inspection.
Vendor consolidation has been a structural trend in cyber for some time, though not
necessarily linear. That said, the current environment may accelerate the
consolidation tailwinds as customers look to close security gaps that may arise from
a fragmented tool stack.
See further commentary from DB’s company research team here.

Our March 2026 “personal investor” survey showed a more positive view
among “ESG” investors. 58% in the US and 52% in the UK saw cybersecurity/data
privacy as an opportunity. Among all investors, US respondents favoured
opportunity over risk (46% vs 30%). UK views were more balanced (38% vs 40%).

Appendix A - Attack types
Top Attack types What it is How AI is helping attackers
Malware Software built to damage, disrupt or gain unauthorised access AI writes harmful software faster. Google has found malware that rewrites its own code while running to avoid detection, and criminal marketplaces selling AI malware-building tools.
Phishing Deceptive messages that trick people into giving up sensitive information Where AI’s effect is clearest: fluent, personalised lures without the old spelling and phrasing giveaways. Google says state-backed groups use AI to mass-produce them, and AI can fake voices and video.

Denial of service (DoS) Flooding a system so it cannot serve legitimate users Little reported evidence so far. AI can help find weak points and automate traffic floods, but the attack itself stays fairly simple.
Distributed denial of service
(DDoS)
The same attack launched from many
compromised devices at once
AI can accelerate the development and automation of attack tooling, while large-scale
botnets provide the distributed capacity behind hyper-volumetric DDoS attacks.

Man-in-the-middle (MitM) Intercepting or altering traffic between two parties Mainly indirect. AI-written phishing pages and deepfake voices make it easier to trick people into connecting through an attacker or approving fake sign-ins.
SQL injection Malicious SQL queries injected to manipulate backend database operations AI tools scan sites and spot flaws in database code faster, so less-skilled attackers can find weaknesses that once needed real expertise.

Cross-site scripting (XSS) Scripts injected into web pages to hijack user sessions As with SQL injection: AI speeds up finding vulnerable pages and writing the malicious code.
Zero-day exploits Attacking a software flaw before a patch is released A major new development. In May 2026 Google reported the first zero-day it believes was developed with AI. Its September 2026 report says the bigger effect is attackers misusing known flaws faster, before fixes land.

Advanced persistent threats
(APTs)
An intruder staying hidden in a network for long-
term
Google reports state-backed groups using AI across the whole attack, from target
research and lures to tooling and sifting stolen data.
Ransomware Encrypting data or locking device and demanding payment to release it NCSC expects AI to add to the global ransomware threat by lowering the skill needed to break in. AI also helps criminals find the most extortable data.

IoT exploitation Abusing weakly secured connected devices for entry AI can scan the internet for unpatched cameras, routers and storage devices and identify which flaws they carry.
Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, National Cyber Security Centre, Google Threat Intelligence Group, Cloudflare, FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3)

Appendix B - European vs US cyber regulation
Europe US Federal
Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026
Regulation Key requirements
NIS2 Directive In force 2023, transposition deadline Oct-24. Covers 18
sectors (up from seven) and creates an EU vulnerability
database. In-scope firms must manage cyber and
supply-chain risk and notify authorities of significant
incidents. Management bodies must approve and
oversee cyber risk measures, with potential liability for

infringements.
Cybersecurity Act In force 2019. Establishes an EU-wide certification
framework for ICT products, services, processes and
managed security services, and made the EU
cybersecurity body ENISA permanent.
Cyber Resilience Act In force 2024, main obligations from 2027.
Manufacturers and retailers of products with digital
elements must deliver security by design, a defined
support period and security fixes within it, plus
mandatory vulnerability management and disclosure.

Digital Operational
Resilience Act (DORA)
In force 2023, applies from 2025. Financial-services
specific, covering own-firm and third-party risk. Requires
an ICT business continuity policy, regular resilience
testing and disclosure of major incidents, and gives
regulators oversight of critical ICT providers.
Cyber Solidarity Act In force 2025. Creates a European Cybersecurity Alert
System to detect threats and share cross-border
warnings, plus a Cyber Emergency Mechanism to boost

preparedness and response.
Regulation Key requirements
White House Accord on
Super Intelligence
(2026)
Emphasises self-regulation to ensure AI safety, with a call for
external evaluations and the possibility of formal regulation in
future. Not legally binding; signed by President Trump and
leading AI companies.
Executive Order on
Cybersecurity (2025)
Highlights risk from foreign actors and orders further action
on defending digital infrastructure, securing the most vital

and governance.
CIRCIA (passed 2022,
not yet in effect)
In-scope entities, focused on critical infrastructure, must
report covered incidents to CISA within 72 hours and
ransomware payments within 24 hours of payment.
Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act
(amended 2024)
Financial institutions must explain information-sharing
practices and safeguard customer data, running a security
programme covering risk assessment, service-provider
oversight and incident response.

Federal Information
Security Modernization
Act (2014)
Applies to federal agencies and contractors: system security
plans, continuous monitoring, incident response capability
and mandatory incident reporting.
Federal Acquisition
Regulation (cyber
provisions 2016)
Governs federal procurement, setting baseline cyber
requirements for suppliers so cyber risk is built into
government contracting. Agencies may impose stricter
provisions.

PREVIEW AND ANALYSIS
Complete Montly CPI will take some time to understand
19 December 2025
Key points
• The October monthly CPI was 
flat in the month, broadly as we 
expected but stronger than market 
expectations. For more information see 
“Electrical noise is far from static”.
• The impact on our inflation profile 
was marginal. The December quarter 
CPI remains 0.6%qtr/3.6%yr, while 
the Trimmed Mean edged up from 
0.7%qtr/3.1%yr to 0.8%qtr/3.2%yr.
• Our November monthly CPI estimate 
is 0.4% for the month, leaving the 
annual pace unchanged at 3.8%yr.
• The introduction of the complete 
monthly CPI is a significant enhancement 
to our analysis of price formation in 
the Australian economy. However, 
with many series having only a short 
history, it will take time to fully 
understand some of the finer details.
• For now, we will continue to place 
greater emphasis on the quarterly 
measures of core inflation.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
No surprise in the October CPI
The new complete monthly CPI gained 3.8% in the year to 
October, coming in a touch softer than Westpac’s estimate of 
3.9%yr but a bit stronger than the market estimate of 3.6%yr. 
So while the market and other commentators may have been 
surprised by the result it was broadly within our expectations 
and as such has had just a marginal impact on our inflation 
profile.
The October CPI was flat in the month, a touch softer than 
Westpac’s published near-cast of 0.1% on the back of a larger 
than expected decline in electricity (–10.2%) which was only 
partially offset by a smaller than expected decline in auto fuel 
(–0.9%) and an unexpected bump up in garments (+2.6%).
The Trimmed Mean (TM) lifted 0.3% in the month of October, 
the same monthly increase it has seen for the previous two 
months and down from the 0.5%mth increase in July but 
stronger than the 0.2%mth prints from March to June.
This saw the annual pace of the TM increase to 3.3% from 
3.2% in September. This month the TM excluded the annual 
rise in electricity; alongside other large price rises and falls. 
Due to the short history of many series in the monthly CPI, the 
annual pace of monthly TM inflation can only be calculated 
back to April 2025. Before then the ABS notes that the annual 
pace is calculated from the quarterly CPI data.
For more information on the October monthly CPI see 
“Electrical noise is far from static ”.
Monthly CPI points to some momentum
Following the October CPI we have seen some minor revisions 
to the various components in our December quarter CPI 
estimate with the upward revisions, such as transport via auto 
fuel and motor vehicles, being offset by downward revisions 
to recreation, alcohol & tobacco and utilities in particular. This 
has left our December CPI estimate unchanged at 0.6%qtr.   
As our Chief Economist, Luci Ellis, has noted that inflation 
did see a bump in the September quarter and again in the 
month of October. However, the main sources of the surprise 
have had little to do with domestic demand or labour market 
pressures. Rather, a sizeable part of the bump looks to have 
been from administered prices and noise. Since the release of 
the data, the RBA has recognised this issue but at the post 
RBA Board Decision Media Conference in December, the 
Governor communicated that the Bank is more worried about 
upside risks to inflation than first thought.
We agree that some of this momentum will continue into the 
December quarter as highlighted by our upward revision in our 
We will continue to focus on the quarterly prints
Justin Smirk
Senior Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
December quarter TM estimate to 0.8%qtr/3.2%yr from our 
earlier estimate of 0.7%qtr/3.1%yr. 
For the December quarter, price changes that are included in 
the TM go from a low of –0.26% to a high of 1.66%. Readers 
will remember that the TM is calculated from seasonally 
adjusted price changes and not the original estimates so if 
unaffected by regular seasonal price shifts. We estimate 
that the most significant components to be trimmed off 
the top in the quarter are: electricity (2.7%); other services 
for motor vehicles (2.5%); tobacco (2.1%); and automotive 
fuel (1.7%). Trimmed off the bottom are: other non-durable 
household products (–0.39%); garments for women (–0.92%); 
international holiday travel (–0.97%); vegetables (–1.16%) and 
wine (–1.27%).   
However, we do not expect this momentum to continue 
through 2026. As Luci noted in her article “ RBA on hold 
in 2026, risks on both sides ” we expect inflation to keep 
easing, due to the lagged impact of past restrictive policy 
and the unwind of earlier government support measures. We 
believe that many commentators have overlooked these lags. 
Monetary policy typically takes a year or more to fully affect 
jobs and prices, so current outcomes reflect the peak cash rate 
from a year ago and we expect to see further labour market 
softening. Strong hiring across the care economy has masked 
some of that restrictiveness, as has public infrastructure 
spending, but these supports are now fading along with last 
year’s tax-cut boost. We believe that even with the 75bps in  
rate cuts this year, policy remains mildly restrictive, and given 
usual lags, this will keep showing in the data through 2026.
Taking a look at some of the recent larger moves in monthly 
prices can highlight many of the issues Luci raises. Taking the 
seasonally adjusted data, smoothing it with a three month 
average then calculating the change over three months (so we 
have three months on three months percentage change) we 
find at the top end of the list some price increases you would 
not expect to continue at the same pace. 
For example at the top are: lamb & goat (8.1%, highly 
seasonal); accessories (8.1% with jewellery prices surging 
on the back of record high gold prices); audio visual & 
computing services (6.0% due to Netflix subscriptions); beef 
& veal (3.7%, highly seasonal); coffee & tea (3.6% noting 
global coffee prices have passed their peak and are already 
significantly lower); water & sewerage (2.3% IPART decision 
was for a 13.8% increase for Sydney Water in October); 
property rates & charges (2.3% administered prices); and 
electricity (1.2%, reflecting unwinding of cost of living 
rebates). Offsetting some of these increases was falling prices 
for: men’s footwear (–2.0%); vegetables (–2.0% seasonal); 
eggs (–1.5%, reflecting the unwinding of the price spike 
associated with an outbreak of bird flu); and major household 
appliances (–1.2%). 
Core inflation still to moderate through 2026
We are projecting headline inflation to get back to the RBA’s 
target band, but not until later in 2026. We now see core 
inflation back below the midpoint of the 2–3% range at the 
end of the year. We had thought this might be achieved by the 
third quarter but now expect current momentum to continue

Electricity to boost November CPI
Westpac is estimating a 0.4% increase in the November

monthly CPI which will hold the annual pace flat at 3.8%yr.
The seasonally adjusted estimate is 0.5% in the month
suggesting it is a stronger than usual estimate.
Boosting the November estimate is: a 16% surge in electricity
(as the impact of the last round of rebates fades); a 3.2% jump
in gas & other household fuels; and a 2.3% bump in auto fuel
(based on reported pump prices). Partially offsetting these
gains is a –2.6% decline in holiday travel & accommodation

and a –2.9% drop in clothing & footwear.
Take care with the Monthly CPI
Many series in the monthly CPI only date back to early 2024
so seasonal adjustment is somewhat problematic. Some
series did have a longer monthly history coming from the
previously published monthly CPI indicator and it appears
the ABS may have used some of this to seasonally adjust the
data. However, we caution that some of the new data has a
different history to the old data and as such, we expect it is

going to take some time to understand the seasonal behaviour
of the new data.
For example, for male garments there is some difference
with the reported monthly change compared to the old CPI
Indicator series but this appears to be only marginal. However,
for women’s, the difference can be significant with even a
reversal of the sign of change in some recent months. And for
some series with a short history and no monthly indicator data
to compare to some of the adjustment appears to be a little

arbitrary, as you can see in the chart right which compares the
children’s garments index in original and seasonally adjusted
terms. The only adjustment made to the series was in June
2025 when a –3.2% change was adjusted to a flat print. Why?
We are yet to uncover.
You can also see where the ABS has turned to smoothing
processes to seasonally adjust short data series. In the chart
over the seasonally adjusted series looks as if has been
generated by applying a Henderson Trend to the original

children’s footwear series.
As such, we expect it is possible there will be significant
adjustments made to the seasonally adjustment process as
data is collected which could result in significant revisions to
seasonally adjusted series including the Trimmed Mean and
the Weighted Median.
BULLETIN

《公債利率攀升，信用市場波動放大》
近期隨著公債利率攀升 、利率波動放大 ，成熟市
場債市信用利差皆有所擴大 ，惟目前利差水準距
上半年貿易戰擔憂時期高點仍有一段距離；不過
現階段貨幣政策不確定性仍將持續使利率波動加
大、市場重新定價貨幣政策預期 ，在今年剩餘的
幾個月中信用利差仍有進一步擴大可能；另一方
面預期市場將衡量債券發行人抵禦高利環境的能
力，故發行人獲利能力以及現金流的強弱或將
市場投資展望

重點摘要 左右市場表現 ，這將體現在高低評等發行人不僅基
本面的分化在高利環境下將更明顯 ，價格表現也將
更分歧。
《非投資級債信用基本面大致穩健，但分歧加大》
第二季美非投資級債發行人獲利狀況強勁 ，除交通
運輸、住房、電信和有線電視產業外 ，所有產業的
EBITDA(息稅折舊攤銷前淨利 )皆有成長，債務槓桿
出現六季以來首次下降 ，利息覆蓋率也提升至兩年
來的最高水準 ，但槓桿率和利息覆蓋率仍分別比指
數長期平均高出 0.17倍以及低 0.26倍，主要原因為
總債務成長 ，以及高利率環境下再融資成本更高 ，
令利息成本上升 。此外美非投資級債發行人過去 12
個月的資本支出年增幅創2023年第三季以來的最高
水準，主要由科技業帶動；不過在整體穩健的資產

負債表狀況中仍有 K型分化的情形 ，如43%的發行
人利息覆蓋率超過五倍 ，此比率為兩年新高 ，但同
時有21%的發行人利息覆蓋率低於 2倍，這也創了
近五年新高 ，反映在高利環境下債信基本面分化加
大，雖然目前違約狀況持穩 ，整體美非投資級債發
行人債信基本面在獲利支持下仍穩健 ，不過在利率
將在高檔區間維持一段時間的預期下 ，基本面的分
化有機會進一步加大 ，此外觀察過去歷史數據顯示
利息保障倍數與利率環境密切相關 ，如美歐信用債
利息保障倍數雖在2022年疫後復甦時期創近年高點 ，
但隨後又隨著利率走高而回落 ，因此儘管近期如上
述利息保障倍數出現小幅改善跡象 ，但基於美歐公
債利率的上升將增加債券再融資帶來的償債成本壓
力，預期債信指標未來也將承壓。

Document 482: 凱基投顧_Takeaway_6257_3265 矽格_台星科_潘俊宏_20261006

Source PDF: all report pdf/凱基投顧_Takeaway_6257_3265 矽格_台星科_潘俊宏_20261006.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-06
Pages: 4
Status: ok

### Page 1

十月 06, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R 1

法說重點摘要

矽格&台星科 
(6257 TT, NT$286, 未評等) & 
(3265 TT, NT$197.5, 未評等)

重點摘要  
1. 矽格以 IC 測試為主，聚焦 AI ASIC 、CPU/GPU 及矽光子等高階測
試。台星科主要提供 晶圓凸塊、覆晶封裝與晶圓測試。矽格目前持
有台星科股權約 51.9% 。 
2. 台星科和矽格連袂調高 2026 年資本支出，其中台星科調高至 134
億元(年增 705%)，矽格追加到 88 億元(年增 66%)，預示了整個產
業產能吃緊，並且 AI 外溢需求已經大幅排擠了原本封測產能。 AI
相關的需求特別是伺服器 CPU 以及矽光子的需求，將大幅帶動公
司明年的業績成長。矽格目前的 2027 年營收展望為年增 30% 以
上。 
3. 不單是產品組合優化，矽格對於產業的價格趨勢也是正面看法，而
資本支出的選擇則是不大幅增加折舊的前提下謹慎規劃，並且有部
分大客戶有預定產能，拉高了整體的訂單能見度。我們對於整體封
測產業在 AI 的帶動之下維持正面且樂觀的看法。矽格目前交易在
2027 年 EPS 市場共識值的 24 倍。

矽格營運概況 
 2Q26 營收 58.8 億元，年增 23.1% ，營業利益 15.1 億元，年增
34.4% ，歸屬母公司淨利 12.2 億元，年增 264% ，EPS 2.55 元。 
 2026 年 1-8 月營收占比 為 AI CPU/GPU 、ASIC 及矽光子 24%、智
慧型手機 30%、車用/醫療 6%、網通/IoT  21%、消費電子/ 智慧家
庭 19%。此外，記憶體占約 10%，營收年增近 30%。 
 2026 年 CapEx 提高至 88 億元，較原始規畫增加 48%，7 月子公
司矽格聯測追加 21 億元，主要擴充美系 CPU/GPU 客戶測試產能。  
矽格未來展望 
 4Q26 動能來自 AI 伺服器、 AI Connectivity 、矽光子、 2 奈米高階
手機晶片、低軌衛星及高速網通；目標 2027 年 AI CPU/GPU 、
ASIC 及矽光子占比超過 30%。 
 湖口二廠目前產能開出 40%，預計 2027 年逐步開滿；中興三廠預
計 4Q26 完成廠房工程、1Q27 量產，主要支援 AI ASIC 、高速網通
及高階晶片需求。  
台星科營運概況 
 2Q26 營收 14.1 億元，年增 20.2%，營業利益 3.38 億元，年增
35%，歸屬母公司淨利 2.85 億，年增 217% ，EPS 2.09 元 
 2026 年 1-8 月 營收占比 為 HPC 49% 、 Memory  8% 、 CE/3C  
34%、IoT 9%，預期 2027-2028 年 HPC 占比持續提升。

台星科未來展望 
 N2 產品認證完成並陸續量產， 55×55mm FCBGA 已進入量產，預
計 2027 年放量。 
 CP 為 2027 年後主要擴充產品線，聚焦 AI Data Center 及 N2 CPU
晶圓測試。 FT 主要對應 AI Server/CoWoS ，涵蓋 Burn-In 及 SLT，
亦為 2027 年成長動能。

矽格 Q&A 
 光通訊相關布局及營收、獲利貢獻展望？   
 矽格布局全方位 AI Connectivity 及 One-stop 光通訊測試服務，
涵蓋測試技術、設備、工程至量產，預期 2027 年光通訊營收倍
數成長，光通訊獲利優於傳統測試。  
 頎邦進入光測試對矽格的影響 ? 
 頎邦主要仍以 Bumping 為主，部分客戶於頎邦完成 Bumping
後，後續測試仍交由矽格 。 
 目前訂單能見度？  
 能見度高，多數主要客戶已簽訂一年以上的產能保障協議。  
 AI ASIC 、矽光子及高速運算 2027 年營收占比？  
 2026 年目前占比約 24%，2027 年目標超過 30%。 
 因應 AI 晶片測試需求提升， 2027 年如何擴充產能？  
 會先與主要客戶確認未來產能需求，並簽訂產能保障協議後再進
行投資，湖口二廠即因客戶需求強勁、自建廠房時程過長，故 提
前購入以銜接產能。  
 2026 年 CapEx 是否還會上修？ 
 目前仍有數個重要客戶洽談合作，若客戶需求非常緊迫，不排除
未來數月再上修 CapEx 以滿足需求。  
 如何因應客戶要求分散生產據點？  
 目前矽格在台灣有 12 座工廠、中國 2 座、日本新增 1 座工廠；
暫無赴美設廠計劃，但 公司以服務客戶為主，只要有需求，皆可
評估於不同地區布局 。 
 湖口二廠滿載後產值多少？ 
 約 80%即可視為接近滿載，滿載後單月營收估約 2-3 億元。 
 中興三廠量產後營收規模？ 
 中興三廠共七層，其中五層規劃為 clean room ，每層約可容納
100 台設備，產能會逐年爬坡，不會在一年內達滿載。 
 湖口二廠是否專門測試 EIC/PIC ？不同客戶設備能否共用？ 
 湖口二廠不限特定產品或客戶； EIC/PIC 測試設備主要為公司自
製，僅需依不同客戶需求微調，因此設備共用性高。

### Page 3

台灣 矽格&台星科

十月 06, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R 3

 如何看 2027 年測試價格？  
 AI 及光通訊晶片複雜度提升後，測試與良率的重要性增加，矽格
表示目前測試價格仍 在漲價。

台星科 Q&A 
 3Q26 營收受到客戶 wafer 供應不及影響，是否會遞延至 4Q26 ？  
 影響主要來自 N2 及 N3 產品，3Q26 未到位部分預計於 4Q26 回
補。 
 目前 CapEx 大幅增加，未來轉換為營收的效率及主要應用為何？  
 新增 CapEx 以測試設備占比較高，主要對應 Data Center 產品，
包括 CPU、ASIC 及矽光相關應用 
 設備交期最長可達約一年，因此 capex 下單與營收貢獻通常存在
至少半年至一年時間差，主要營收貢獻將落在 2027 年。 
 是否可以用 1 元 CapEx 對應約 0.5 元營收估算？  
 針對公司 2027 年以晶圓測試為主的投資，管理層確認上述估算
合理。

Document 496: 华尔街警惕贝森特再度出手压制收益率，本周五或现关键线索

Source PDF: all report pdf/华尔街警惕贝森特再度出手压制收益率，本周五或现关键线索.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-15
Pages: 3

### Page 1

本周五，主要国债⼀级交易商将在再融资公告发布前收到美国财政部的例⾏调查问卷。花
旗预计问卷或暗⽰削减⻓端发债规模，甚⾄取消 20 年期美债发⾏。摩根⼠丹利则强调： " 接
下来的两次再融资公告将⾄关重要，尤其是 11 ⽉那次，正好紧接中期选举之后。 "
摘要：
美国国债市场正密切关注财政部的下⼀步动作。
本周五，主要国债⼀级交易商将在再融资公告发布前收到财政部的例⾏调查问卷。市场⼈⼠认
为，问卷的提问⽅式和内容本⾝，可能成为判断⻉森特政策倾向的⻛向标。
据花旗集团分析，此次问卷或暗⽰⻓端发债规模将有所收缩，⻉森特可能以养⽼基⾦对⻓端国
债需求不振为由，考虑削减乃⾄取消 20 年期国债发⾏。
摩根⼠丹利投资管理⼴泛市场固定收益主管 Vishal Khanduja 表⽰：
借贷成本上升⼀直是特朗普政府的⼼腹之患，⻉森特 8 ⽉的 " 财政部扭曲操作 " 初期确实压制了收
益率，但也实际上打破了财政部数⼗年坚守的 " 定期且可预测 " 发债原则，引发市场对政策不确定
性加剧的忧虑。
对于投资者⽽⾔，财政部的下⼀步选择将直接影响美国国债市场的供需格局与定价逻辑。

财政部多套⽅案悬⽽未决
据摩根⼠丹利银⾏业策略师 Martin Tobias 分析，美国财政部⽬前⾯临多种选择。
最为保守的⽅案是维持现状，延续 8 ⽉的做法，⾄少在未来⼏个季度内保持拍卖规模不变，不修
改前瞻指引。
另⼀选项是修订发债前瞻指引，明确排除对 20 年期和 30 年期等⻓端债券的增发，转⽽将增发重
⼼集中于 2 ⾄ 7 年期中短端品种。
最为⾮常规的⼀种⽅案，则是明确设定短期国库券在整体发债中的占⽐上限。官员此前从未就
这⼀⽐例设定上限，⽽⽬前短期国库券占⽐已攀升⾄ 22% 以上。
Columbia Threadneedle Investments 投资组合经理 Ed Al-Hussainy 认为，从纳税⼈利益出发，
最优⽅案是将政府融资重⼼转移⾄ 5 年期附近，⽽⾮延伸⾄ 30 年期。他说：
削减⻓端供给引发账单替代⽅案讨论
你已经向市场表明，这样的收益率⽔平是有问题的。因此我们认为，接下来的两次再融资公告将
⾄关重要，尤其是 11 ⽉那次，正好紧接中期选举之后。
如果你的⽬标是遏制收益率上⾏，那就必须有⾜够的创意。
